# Policy text to detector (challenge 3)

Generated by `python -m kavach.rules all` (kavach/rules.py) at 2026-10-30T01:22:42 on dataset version `eb6caf5672c6`. The rule sentences are read from the organizer's policy file at run time; nothing about them is pasted into code. Raw results, every hit and every comparison row: `detectors/rules/<rule>.json`.

Pipeline: the sentence is tokenized by a small lexicon (counts, windows, money, size words, channels, card scope, sequence words, entities, predicates, states, actions, conditions); the tokens pick a detector template (single-card sequence or cross-card shared element); every gap the prose leaves becomes a recorded choice; the compiled rule object exposes `fire(card_history_df, anchor)`. The backtest re-runs the detector once per alternative reading, one choice at a time, so the table below each rule shows what each ambiguity costs.

Re-run any claim: `python -m kavach.rules check <R5|R6> <CC-xxxx|HHF-xxx>` (one case) or `python -m kavach.rules <rule>` (the whole backtest and comparison).

## R5. Card testing

> Three or more small online authorizations on one card within an hour, followed by a larger purchase: recommend `DECLINE_TRANSACTION` and `STEP_UP_AUTH`. If a purchase over $100 has already cleared, recommend `BLOCK_CARD`.

Source: `data/raw/round2/policy/fraud_policy.md` (sha256 `58c4cc3b5fe9200b`). Template: `single_card_sequence`.

### Prose to detector

- Read and parse: 0.4 ms; interpretation (including the data-measured choices): 20.3 ms; compile to a working `fire()` and run it on a first closed case: 45.0 ms; to a full backtest: 154.3 s.
- Tokens: `Three or more` -> count; `small` -> size (open); `online` -> channel; `authorizations` -> event (open); `on one card` -> scope; `within an hour` -> window; `followed by` -> sequence (open); `larger` -> size (open); `purchase` -> event; ``DECLINE_TRANSACTION`` -> action; ``STEP_UP_AUTH`` -> action; `If a purchase over $100 has already cleared,` -> condition; `purchase` -> event; `over $100` -> money; `has already cleared` -> state (open); ``BLOCK_CARD`` -> action.
- Executable parameters: `min_tests` = 3, `test_window_min` = 60, `test_channel` = online, `test_max_amt` = 10.1, `unit` = card, `window_binds` = tests, `follow_gap_h` = 24, `follow_rule` = not_small, `follow_channel` = any, `scope` = 72, `block_over` = 100.0, `branch_state` = cleared.
- Consequence: actions `DECLINE_TRANSACTION`, `STEP_UP_AUTH`; if a purchase over $100 has already cleared: `BLOCK_CARD`.

### What the prose left open, and how it was resolved

| choice | words | kind | taken | alternatives measured | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `small_amount` | 'small' | vague | under $10.10 | under $5.03 (1st percentile); under $15.03 (10th percentile) | The policy gives no number. A label-free reading: an amount is small when it sits in the bottom 5% of what this bank's customers pay online (July to December 2016), which is $10.10; it is computed from the index at compile time, so the rule never learned from the case labels it is scored on. It also matches the agent's own card-testing detector (under $10), which keeps the rule-versus-agent comparison about structure, not about a dollar figure. Measured: 5th percentile of online transaction amounts before 2017 = 10.10 (`select quantile_cont(TransactionAmt, 0.05) from txn where channel = ? and ts < ?` with ['online', '2017-01-01'], 2026-10-30T01:08:05). |
| `window_binding` | 'within an hour' | vague | the small authorizations only | the whole sequence, larger purchase included | The comma reads 'Three or more small ... within an hour, followed by a larger purchase': the window phrase sits inside the first clause, before the comma, so it counts the tests; the cash-out is attached by 'followed by'. |
| `follow_gap` | 'followed by' | vague | within 24 hours | within 1 hour; within 72 hours | The text sets no gap. R5's remedy is to decline pending authorizations and require step-up before further activity, which only makes sense while the cash-out is imminent; a day keeps it the same episode, and the episode builder's short lookback is 3 days (sensitivity shows 1 and 72 hours). |
| `larger_purchase` | 'larger' | vague | not small | over 5 times the largest test and over $20 (the agent's bar); over $100 (the rule's own block threshold) | 'larger' is a comparative and its referent is the small authorizations, so the literal reading is the opposite of small: any purchase at or above the small threshold is larger than every test. The agent's stricter bar (5 times and $20) and the rule's own $100 block threshold are measured as alternatives. |
| `authorization` | 'authorizations' | schema | every transaction row | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | The index has one row per approved transaction and no authorization or settlement status; declined test attempts are invisible, so the rule can only under-count tests. Recorded as a schema gap, no alternative is possible. |
| `follow_channel` | 'purchase' | omitted | any channel | online only | 'online' qualifies the small authorizations; the larger purchase carries no channel word, so it is not restricted. |
| `one_card` | 'on one card' | schema | the card id | the account behind the card (holder_key, usable before 2017 only) | The card id is what the alert, the case and the answer name. In this data a card id pools many accounts (holder_key = card1, billing region and account start), so a pooled card can show three small online purchases in an hour from three different people; the account reading is measured as the alternative, but it cannot be the default because holder_key is unusable from January 2017 (D1 resampled, january-data-profile.md), the month under investigation. |
| `alert_scope` | (the rule is silent) | omitted | within 72 hours either side | the flagged transaction is itself a test or the larger purchase; a sequence within 24 hours either side | R5 reacts to pending authorizations and to a purchase that has already cleared, i.e. to the card's activity around the alert, not to the flagged row alone. 72 hours equals the episode builder's short lookback (kavach.episode_model LOOKBACK_DAYS = 3). |
| `knowledge_cutoff` | (the rule is silent) | omitted | up to opened_at | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | A detector may not use activity after the decision. Case anchors stop at opened_at; reference anchors, which have no case, stop at the anchor plus the median delay between a closed case's first fraudulent transaction and its opened_at, so both sides see the same amount of future on average. |
| `already_cleared` | 'a purchase over $100 has already cleared' | schema | dated at or before the decision | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | The schema has no clearing status; a recorded transaction dated before the decision is treated as cleared (approved rows only are recorded). |

Reading: as written and resolved, R5 does not separate fraud from normal traffic: the shape is at least as common around a random transaction as around the first transaction of a confirmed fraud (lift 0.45, 3 cleared customers acted against). The most consequential open choice is `alert_scope`: reading it as 'the flagged transaction is itself a test or the larger purchase' moves the lift to 0.84 and the cleared hits to 0.

`*` the reference sample had no hit, so the lift uses 0.5 hits as the floor and is an upper reading.

## R6. Shared origin

> When several cards show fraud from the same device profile, the same billing region, or the same recipient email in one window, name the shared element, recommend `CREATE_CASE` and `FILE_REPORT`, and `MONITOR_CONNECTED_CARDS` for every card that shares it.

Source: `data/raw/round2/policy/fraud_policy.md` (sha256 `58c4cc3b5fe9200b`). Template: `cross_card_shared_element`.

### Prose to detector

- Read and parse: 0.5 ms; interpretation (including the data-measured choices): 10.9 ms; compile to a working `fire()` and run it on a first closed case: 28.1 ms; to a full backtest: 72.9 s.
- Tokens: `several` -> count (open); `several cards` -> scope; `show fraud` -> predicate (open); `the same device profile` -> entity (open); `the same billing region` -> entity (open); `the same recipient email` -> entity (open); `in one window` -> window (open); `name the shared element` -> output; ``CREATE_CASE`` -> action; ``FILE_REPORT`` -> action; ``MONITOR_CONNECTED_CARDS`` -> action; `for every card that shares it` -> target (open).
- Executable parameters: `min_cards` = 3, `fields` = ['device_key', 'addr1', 'R_emaildomain'], `window_days` = 14, `evidence` = either, `baseline_ratio` = 3.0, `baseline_days` = 90, `region_channel` = any, `rare_cards` = 100.
- Consequence: actions `CREATE_CASE`, `FILE_REPORT`, `MONITOR_CONNECTED_CARDS`; outputs: shared_element; the last action targets all_sharing.

### What the prose left open, and how it was resolved

| choice | words | kind | taken | alternatives measured | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `several` | 'several' | vague | 3 or more | 2 or more; 4 or more | 'Several' means more than two. The bank's own ring note (CC-2649: 'Two other cardholders reported the same device profile this month') treats three cards as a ring, and the agent's ring detectors use the same minimum (kavach.detectors.network.RING_MIN_CARDS = 3). |
| `show_fraud` | 'show fraud' | vague | precedent or atypical | atypical only; precedent only; atypical, a device marked New also counting | Fraud is only confirmed in the closed cases, so a confirmed case opened before the decision (not the alert's own) is the strongest reading; but a ring's newest victims have no case yet, so an element transaction that is atypical for the card's own history also counts (first use of the product, amount above every earlier one overall or for that product, first proxied connection: kavach.duckq_ext.card_atypical_window, the flags the typology detectors use). A device marked New is left out by default: it is common in normal traffic (onecard.py notes it does not separate fraud for most products). |
| `device_profile` | 'the same device profile' | schema | identical device_key, rare profiles only | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | device_key joins DeviceInfo, OS, browser and screen. A placeholder ('|||') or a family name such as 'Windows' or 'iOS Device' is shared by thousands of phones and is not one device: those are excluded with the agent's own test (kavach.detectors.network.generic_device: empty, on more than 100 cards, or a family name). |
| `billing_region` | 'the same billing region' | schema | addr1 on every row | addr1 on card-present rows only | addr1 is the billing region of the cardholder on every row; the rule says billing region, not place of purchase, so the literal reading keeps online rows (a regional breach shows up there). The typology detectors' card-present reading is measured as the alternative. |
| `email` | 'the same recipient email' | schema | the same R_emaildomain | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | The index stores email domains, not addresses, so 'the same email' can only be the same domain; for free-mail domains this is weak, which the baseline condition below handles. |
| `window` | 'in one window' | vague | 14 days either side | 7 days either side; 3 days either side; 30 days either side | The text gives no length. The bank's own confirmed ring ran 17.5 days from its first to its last confirmed card (the SM-G935F ring, CC-2649 to CC-3035), and an alert can come late in a ring, so the window must hold about two weeks of the ring's history before the alert; with the cutoff at the decision time, 14 days either side is in practice the 14 days before the alert. The agent's 7-day network window and 3 and 30 days are measured as alternatives. Measured: longest span, in days, between the first confirmed-fraud transactions of the cards of one bank ring (closed cases listing connected cards) = 17.54 (`with ring as (select c.case_id, unnest(string_split(c.card_id || '|' || c.connected_card_ids, '|')) card from closed c where c.connected_card_ids is not null and c.connected_card_ids <> ''), m as (select r.case_id, r.card, min(t.ts) first_ts from ring r join closed_txn ct on ct.card_id = r.card and ct.outcome = 'confirmed_fraud' join closed cc on cc.case_id = ct.case_id and cc.pattern = 'undocumented' join txn t on t.TransactionID = ct.TransactionID group by 1, 2) select max(span) from (select case_id, date_diff('hour', min(first_ts), max(first_ts)) / 24.0 span from m group by 1)` with [], 2026-10-30T01:20:55). |
| `common_element` | (the rule is silent) | omitted | at least 3 times the baseline | no baseline condition; at least 5 times the baseline | The rule is silent, but 'the same billing region' or 'the same recipient email' identifies a shared origin only when more cards show fraud from it than usually do: gmail.com and the largest regions carry dozens of atypical purchases every week. The baseline is the mean number of cards showing fraud from that value per window over the 90 days before the window (complete windows inside the data only), scaled to the part of the window the cutoff leaves; with no baseline the value must be rare (100 cards or fewer in the window). |
| `anchor_card` | (the rule is silent) | omitted | yes | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | The alert is the reason to look; its card carries the element through the flagged transaction, and R6 is applied to that card's case. The other cards must show fraud on their own evidence. |
| `elements_of` | (the rule is silent) | omitted | the flagged transaction's values | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | R6 names the shared element of the alert; the flagged transaction's device profile, billing region and recipient domain are the candidates (earlier rows of the card would add values the alert is not about). |
| `every_card` | 'for every card that shares it' | vague | all sharing cards for a rare element, else the fraud-showing ones | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | Taken literally for gmail.com the rule would put thousands of customers under monitoring; monitoring costs the customer nothing, but an analyst cannot act on thousands of names. Both counts are reported per hit. |
| `knowledge_cutoff` | (the rule is silent) | omitted | up to opened_at | none (no alternative reading exists in this schema) | Same cut as R5; the alert's own closed case never counts as precedent for itself. |

### Backtest (closed cases and reference sample, kavach/backtest.py conventions)

| reading | fraud hits | cleared hits | reference hits | lift (95% interval) | precision on closed |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| default | 539 / 4675 | 94 / 910 | 698 / 20000 | 3.30 (2.97 to 3.68) | 0.852 |
| several: 2 or more | 722 / 4675 | 123 / 910 | 900 / 20000 | 3.43 (3.13 to 3.76) | 0.854 |
| several: 4 or more | 416 / 4675 | 82 / 910 | 615 / 20000 | 2.89 (2.57 to 3.26) | 0.835 |
| show_fraud: atypical only | 257 / 4675 | 93 / 910 | 669 / 20000 | 1.64 (1.43 to 1.89) | 0.734 |
| show_fraud: precedent only | 494 / 4675 | 38 / 910 | 405 / 20000 | 5.22 (4.59 to 5.93) | 0.929 |
| show_fraud: atypical, a device marked New also counting | 467 / 4675 | 150 / 910 | 1086 / 20000 | 1.84 (1.66 to 2.04) | 0.757 |
| billing_region: addr1 on card-present rows only | 488 / 4675 | 58 / 910 | 694 / 20000 | 3.01 (2.69 to 3.36) | 0.894 |
| window: 7 days either side | 477 / 4675 | 87 / 910 | 628 / 20000 | 3.25 (2.9 to 3.64) | 0.846 |
| window: 3 days either side | 387 / 4675 | 59 / 910 | 547 / 20000 | 3.03 (2.67 to 3.43) | 0.868 |
| window: 30 days either side | 654 / 4675 | 104 / 910 | 867 / 20000 | 3.23 (2.93 to 3.55) | 0.863 |
| common_element: no baseline condition | 4587 / 4675 | 891 / 910 | 19689 / 20000 | 1.00 (0.99 to 1.0) | 0.837 |
| common_element: at least 5 times the baseline | 515 / 4675 | 94 / 910 | 688 / 20000 | 3.20 (2.87 to 3.57) | 0.846 |

Reading: as written and resolved, R6 separates fraud from normal traffic (lift 3.30, 94 cleared customers acted against). The most consequential open choice is `common_element`: reading it as 'no baseline condition' moves the lift to 1.00 and the cleared hits to 891.

`*` the reference sample had no hit, so the lift uses 0.5 hits as the floor and is an upper reading.

Population: 20,000 transactions before 2017 in no confirmed-fraud case (seeded hash sample, seed 42); backtest run 72.9 s.
